bedrock backendSonnet 5 and Opus 5 · high effort · backend enforced and verified from every result receipt
Cost not recordedThe Bedrock runner does not retain AWS billing usage, so this report does not insert an estimate.
Case 9 follow-upWith the corrected deterministic grader, Sonnet 5 High and Opus 5 High each scored 27.27, 27.27, and 81.82 (mean 45.45; 0/3 strict pass). Four runs stopped after sequential create-validation errors. The two stronger runs created the replacement, verified it ACTIVE with a fresh read, and then paused both source ad sets, but each omitted source-derived delivery fields, so the exact-configuration gate correctly failed. The executor context was sufficient, and this case has no LLM-judge variance. The grader correction only decouples audience-ID checks from unrelated configuration fields.

{final_output}
**Short answer: the CTR increase is a traffic-mix artifact, not a genuine performance improvement.** Blended account CTR rose from 0.78% (Jun 19–Jul 5) to 0.95% (Jul 6–Jul 22), a +0.17pp / +21% relative gain — but decomposing that change shows the underlying campaigns actually got *worse* at the individual level, and the account-level number only looks better because low-CTR video traffic pulled back while higher-CTR search traffic expanded its share.

**Data source**: Google Ads account 4546011805 (customer.currency_code returned "UNKNOWN" via the account/currency lookup, so cost figures below are shown in the account's native reporting units without a currency symbol — treat them as directional only). Analysis covers 5 active campaigns: Bluefunnel_YT_RT_v2 (ID 23951248965, Video), Jul26_Conversion_Search_NonBrand_US (ID 23968488159, Search), Jul26_Competitor_JobSearchTools_US (ID 23981985312, Search), Jul26_Competitor_DirectSwitchers_US (ID 23986959569, Search), Jul26_Competitor_Telugu_US (ID 23991514528, Search).

**What actually happened, campaign by campaign:**

```json
{"table": {"title": "Campaign CTR and Impression Share: Jun 19–Jul 5 vs Jul 6–Jul 22", "headers": ["Campaign", "Channel", "Impr. Share P1", "Impr. Share P2", "CTR P1", "CTR P2", "CTR Direction"], "rows": [["Bluefunnel_YT_RT_v2", "Video", "88.0%", "71.0%", "0.257%", "0.165%", "Worse"], ["Jul26_Conversion_Search_NonBrand_US", "Search", "5.1%", "5.7%", "7.967%", "8.260%", "Slightly better"], ["Jul26_Competitor_JobSearchTools_US", "Search", "4.1%", "12.3%", "1.276%", "0.953%", "Worse"], ["Jul26_Competitor_DirectSwitchers_US", "Search", "2.6%", "9.2%", "2.698%", "2.150%", "Worse"], ["Jul26_Competitor_Telugu_US", "Search", "0.2%", "1.9%", "12.500%", "2.527%", "Much worse"]]}}
```

Four of five campaigns saw their **own CTR decline** in the latest period — only the Non-Brand Search campaign improved (and only marginally, +0.29pp). What changed dramatically is the **impression mix**: the Video campaign (which has an inherently near-zero CTR, ~0.2%) shrank from 88% to 71% of total impressions, while the three Search campaigns with materially higher CTRs collectively grew from 6.9% to 23.4% of impressions. Since Search campaigns convert impressions to clicks at a much higher rate than the Video campaign, simply shifting volume toward them mechanically lifts the blended CTR — regardless of whether performance improved.

**Shift-share decomposition confirms this quantitatively:**

```json
{"chart": {"type": "bar", "title": "CTR Change Decomposition (percentage points)", "data": {"labels": ["Rate Effect (within-campaign CTR change)", "Mix Effect (traffic shift toward Search)", "Total Actual Change"], "datasets": [{"label": "Contribution to CTR change (pp)", "data": [-0.112, 0.49, 0.166]}]}}
```

**This is the key finding: if the traffic mix had stayed exactly as it was in the first period, blended CTR would have *fallen* to 0.67% (rate effect = ‑0.11pp) because individual campaigns got worse at engaging their audience. Instead, the mix shift alone contributed +0.49pp — more than double the actual observed increase — meaning the mix effect is fully masking a real, if small, decline in campaign-level engagement quality.**

Supporting context: total spend rose from ~1,291 to ~2,168 (unit currency unconfirmed) while conversions moved from 0 to 2 across the entire account — too few data points (well below the 5-conversion / 50-click confidence threshold) to call this a genuine conversion-rate improvement; it's more likely incidental. The Bluefunnel video campaign has logged **zero conversions across the full 34-day window** despite consuming the majority of impression volume and spend, and its CTR trend is declining, not stabilizing.

**Bottom line**: Don't read the higher account CTR as validation that campaigns are performing better. It reflects less video-retargeting volume relative to search, not sharper targeting or creative within any individual campaign — and the underlying per-campaign trend is actually negative.

Given the diagnostic nature of this finding, I'm submitting two recommendations below. This account is small in volume (283 clicks total in the latest 17-day window), so any account-level restructuring should be revisited once click/conversion volume grows.

Based on Shopify sessions analytics (`waggle-app.myshopify.com`), the funnel is **not breaking at checkout** — it's breaking upstream, before shoppers ever get there.

Key findings, comparing Jan 1–Jul 30 across three years:

- **Sessions**: 523,839 (2024) → 762,536 (2025) → 909,372 (2026) — traffic is up +73.6% vs. the old (2024) benchmark and +19.3% vs. 2025.
- **Completed checkouts**: 8,694 (2024) → 10,833 (2025) → 8,180 (2026) — down 5.9% vs. 2024 and down 24.5% vs. 2025, despite the traffic surge.
- **Checkout completion rate** (of shoppers who reach checkout): 39.40% (2024) → 32.96% (2025) → 35.77% (2026) — this is close to historical norms and is actually *higher* than last year. Checkout itself is not the bottleneck.
- **Add-to-cart rate**: 4.56% → 4.07% → 3.08% of sessions — declining.
- **Checkout-reach rate** (sessions that start checkout at all): 4.21% → 4.31% → 2.51% of sessions — the steepest drop in the funnel, roughly -40% relative to both prior years.

Digging into the last 30 days of landing-page data explains most of the gap: a single blog post ("heat stroke in dogs") drives 6% of all sessions with essentially zero purchase intent (1 add-to-cart, 0 checkouts). More significantly, the two largest landing pages — `/products/pet-monitor` and `/products/rv-4g-mini-camera` — together account for 37% of traffic but convert at only 0.83% and 0.69%, well below the homepage (1.85%) and the exclusive-deals page (3.70%). Mobile, at 82% of sessions, also reaches checkout at roughly half the rate of desktop (2.28% vs. 3.90%), though its completion rate once at checkout is normal.

**Bottom line**: the traffic surge is real, but it's a lower-intent mix — heavier on content traffic and cold product-page landings, with weaker mobile engagement pre-checkout. Fix the top-of-funnel/product-page engagement problem, not the checkout flow.

Full breakdown (funnel-stage benchmarks, landing-page table, device split, and root-cause narrative) is in the report I've generated: **"Waggle: Where the Checkout Funnel Is Actually Breaking."**

**Reconciliation across reporting levels:**

```json
{"table": {"title": "Search Conversion Reconciliation (May 25 – Jun 23)", "headers": ["Level", "Total Conversions", "Gap vs. Campaign Level"], "rows": [["Campaign (reported total)", "290.97", "—"], ["Ad Group level", "287.97", "3.00 (1.03%) missing"], ["Keyword level (keyword_view)", "287.97", "3.00 (1.03%) missing"]]}}
```

The ad group-level and keyword-level totals match exactly (287.97), which tells us the gap isn't a keyword-vs-ad-group reporting quirk — it's a genuine 3.0-conversion (≈1%) block that Google counts at the campaign aggregate but never assigns down to any ad group or keyword row. Finance cannot trace that slice to any criterion, enabled or not — it's structurally unidentifiable through standard reporting, most consistent with modeled/estimated conversion adjustments (e.g., conversion modeling for consent-gap recovery) that Google applies at the account/campaign level rather than to a specific click.

**For the ~99% that IS keyword-attributed:** every one of the 34 keywords carrying conversions in this window has `ad_group_criterion.status = ENABLED` right now — none trace to a paused or removed keyword. So on the "currently enabled keyword" test, that portion passes cleanly.

**Where it breaks down is the "actual user search" test.** None of the 34 converting keywords use Exact match — they're exclusively Broad or Phrase match. Broad/Phrase match means Google credits a conversion to the keyword's *concept*, not the literal string the user typed. For example, ad group "GRUPO 01 – RECOLOCAÇÃO" (campaign 21114948220) has one Broad-match keyword, "empresa de recolocação profissional," carrying 70.99 conversions — but the search_term_view report shows those conversions actually came from literal queries as varied as "maturi," "trabalhe conosco," "linkedin," "freelancer," "indeed," and "empresas em barueri contratando." None of those phrases appear anywhere in the enabled keyword text; Google's matching algorithm decided they were relevant.

```json
{"table": {"title": "Example: One Broad-Match Keyword vs. Its Actual Triggering Searches", "headers": ["Ad Group", "Enabled Keyword (Broad)", "Sample Literal Search Terms That Converted"], "rows": [["GRUPO 01 - RECOLOCAÇÃO (21114948220)", "empresa de recolocação profissional", "maturi; trabalhe conosco; linkedin; freelancer; indeed; empresas em barueri contratando"], ["GRUPO 01 - HEAD (21111476111)", "headhunter", "agencias de emprego; indeed; rh porto alegre vagas; page personnel; vagas poa rs"]]}}
```

Additionally, the search-term detail itself is long-tailed: the top 200 search-term rows by conversion volume (sorted descending, conversions > 0) only account for 126.5 of the 288 keyword-attributed conversions — meaning well over half of keyword-level conversions are spread across search terms outside that top-200 window, each contributing small fractional credit (consistent with a non-last-click attribution model splitting conversion credit across multiple query touchpoints). Reconstructing a full, exhaustive term-by-term ledger would require exporting beyond a single 200-row query.

**Net assessment for finance:**
1. ~99% of Search conversions (287.97 of 290.97) tie to a keyword that is currently ENABLED — no paused/removed keywords are absorbing credit.
2. ~1% (3.00 conversions) cannot be tied to any ad group or keyword — Google reports them only at the campaign aggregate.
3. Because converting keywords are 100% Broad/Phrase match (zero Exact), the keyword text is not a reliable proxy for "the actual words the user searched" — actual queries diverge substantially and must be pulled from the search terms report separately, not assumed from keyword text.
4. Account currency could not be confirmed via the API in this session (`currency_code` returned empty/UNKNOWN) — the cost figures above are in whatever currency the account reports natively; confirm with your Google Ads UI billing settings before using them in financial reconciliation.

This data spans 3+ distinct categories (campaign/ad-group/keyword reconciliation, match-type/search-term traceability, and the unattributed-conversion gap) — I can turn this into a formal audit report if useful for sharing with finance.

**Scope of this check:** Account 3202791675, all `SEARCH`-channel campaigns, 2026-05-25 through 2026-06-23. There are 4 Search campaigns in the account; 3 are delivering (campaign IDs 21114948220 "SP", 21111476111 "RS", 21111476063 "BH") and 1 (11503596929, "Teste CPA") had zero impressions/conversions in this window and is excluded.

```json
{"table": {"title": "Search Conversion Attribution Reconciliation (May 25 – Jun 23, 2026)", "headers": ["Layer", "Conversions", "% of Campaign Total", "Notes"], "rows": [["Campaign-level reported total (3 active Search campaigns)", "290.97", "100%", "Source of truth finance sees in the dashboard"], ["Sum tied to a specific keyword row (keyword_view)", "288.97", "99.31%", "34 distinct keyword rows across 8 ad groups"], ["Of that, tied to a keyword whose status is currently ENABLED", "288.97", "99.31%", "100% of matched keyword rows are ENABLED — none came from a paused/removed keyword"], ["Unidentifiable to any keyword", "2.00", "0.69%", "Present in campaign total but has no corresponding keyword_view row"]]}}
```

So the "currently enabled keyword" test passes almost completely — of the conversions that do map to a keyword, every single one maps to a keyword that is ENABLED today. There is no leakage from paused or removed keywords. The gap is the ~2.0 conversions (0.69%) that show up in the campaign total but never appear against any keyword at all in the keyword report for this period — that portion is genuinely unidentifiable at the keyword level.

**Tying keyword → actual user search term introduces two more gaps finance should know about:**

1. **Google withholds low-volume search terms.** The search terms report (`search_term_view`) only itemizes queries that clear Google's internal volume/privacy threshold. Long-tail queries below that threshold are never surfaced individually — they're folded into the aggregate the advertiser can't see. Several of the top-converting keywords driving this account are **Broad match** (e.g., "empresa de recolocação profissional" — 70.99 conversions, 24.4% of the total, campaign 21114948220 / ad group 161656016524; "transição de carreira" — 45.99 conversions, campaign 21111476111 / ad group 196014735986; "headhunter" — 30 conversions, campaign 21111476111 / ad group 163055419114). Broad match means Google's own matching logic decides which literal queries trigger the ad, so a meaningful share of the traffic behind these keywords will never have a visible literal search string tied to it, even though the keyword itself is fully identifiable.

2. **Some "visible" search-term conversions are modeled, not observed.** Multiple rows in the search terms report carry fractional conversion counts rather than whole numbers — for example "como tornar meu linkedin mais atrativo" (1.5), "infojobs porto alegre" (0.994), "vagas em guaiba" (0.992), "suzano mais emprego" (0.989), plus several at exactly 0.5. A fractional count is Google's signal that the conversion is statistically modeled (consent-mode/cross-device estimation) rather than a clean 1:1 observed click-to-conversion event. So even where a literal search term is shown, part of its conversion credit isn't a hard audit trail back to one identifiable user action.

**Bottom line for finance:** 99.3% of reported conversions tie to a currently-enabled keyword, and 100% of the keyword-matched portion is attached to keywords that are active right now — there's no "ghost keyword" problem. But roughly 0.7% cannot be matched to any keyword, and within the matched portion, broad-match keyword structure plus Google's search-term privacy threshold and conversion modeling mean not every conversion can be traced to one specific, literal user search query. Treat the reported total as directionally reliable but not a fully auditable 1:1 chain from dollar spent → keyword → literal search → conversion.

**Why the ad-level views look lower — two separate reasons:**

1. **The unfiltered ad-level export is NOT actually lower once fully reconciled.** Campaign spend is by definition the sum of its ads' spend, so a *complete* ad-level pull must also equal 61,197.95. The account has 259 individual delivering ads, which spans multiple API response pages. If an export tool (or a person in Ads Manager) pulls only the first page of ad rows without paging through to the end, the visible subtotal will understate total spend — not because ad-level data is wrong, but because the pull was incomplete. This is a pagination/completeness issue, not a real discrepancy.

2. **The "Top 90 Ads by Spend" report is a deliberate subset, not a discrepancy.** By construction, that view only ever shows the 90 highest-spending ads out of the account's 259 delivering ads for the week. Reconstructing that leaderboard from the full sorted ad list gives 55,201.00 — exactly 5,996.95 (≈9.8%) below the account total. That gap is the combined spend of the remaining ~169 lower-spend "long-tail" ads that fall outside a top-90 cutoff. This is expected behavior for any Top-N report and should never be used as a stand-in for total account spend.

This campaign does not appear anywhere in the verified 17-campaign breakdown that already reconciles exactly to the account total, so it cannot be legitimate delivery data for this account. I excluded it from all totals above. If this recurs on a live pull, treat it as a data-integrity issue to investigate rather than real spend — do not let it factor into finance reporting.

**Bottom line:** Account total = Campaign total = 61,197.95 (use this for finance). The ad-level unfiltered export reconciles to the same figure once fully paginated; the Top 90 report is intentionally a partial leaderboard and will always run lower by the long-tail amount excluded.

**Why the saved "Top 90 Ads by Spend" report is lower:** That report is a leaderboard, not a census. It's built with `limit=90` sorted by spend descending, but this account delivered on roughly 250 real ads in the window (the campaign total of $61,197.95 rolls up across many more ads than 90). Capping at the top 90 spenders captures the highest-value ads but silently drops the entire long tail of smaller ads — those beyond ad #90 by spend collectively account for $5,996.95, or 9.8% of total account spend. That's why it undercounts against account/campaign totals: it was never designed to be a full spend total, only a ranked subset.

**Why the "unfiltered" ad-level export doesn't reconcile cleanly either:** Pulling ad-level data with no filters required two paginated calls. That campaign ID does not exist anywhere in the 17-campaign campaign-level report or account structure — it's not one of Nutripure FR's real campaigns. This is a data-integrity anomaly, not real spend: an unfiltered ad export can inflate above the true total if it includes orphaned/placeholder rows like this that have no matching parent campaign. I'd flag this specific campaign ID to your Meta support/platform team before treating any "unfiltered" ad-level pull as ground truth — the account and campaign layers remain the trustworthy source.

**Bottom line for finance:** Use $61,197.95 (account = campaign total, exact match). Treat the Top 90 report strictly as "our biggest ads," not total spend — it excludes ~9.8% of spend by design. **How this was calculated — and why it differs from a naive pull.** Google's `search_term_view` returns one row per search term *per ad group*, so the same query can appear multiple times. `wwwargentinaoutcom` came back as three separate rows (28, 12, and 9 clicks). I also pulled the full click-generating term set (all 60 rows, every term with ≥1 click) rather than pre-filtering on clicks, so terms that only cross the 5-click threshold *after* aggregation are correctly captured. The result set was well under the pagination limit, so coverage is complete for the window.

The `wwwargentinaoutcom` term is a navigational query — someone typing your own URL into the search box. At 129.47 it's the single largest zero-conversion line item and the largest by a wide margin, but branded navigational traffic behaves differently from prospecting traffic and its zero-conversion status may reflect attribution rather than wasted spend.

Conversions in this account are recorded fractionally (several terms show values like 2.5 and 0.5), which indicates data-driven or modeled attribution is in use. I treated only an exact aggregate of 0.0 conversions as "no conversions" — terms with partial credit such as `argentina tours from usa` (0.5) were excluded as converting.

### A budget bug I caught mid-execution

The campaign creation silently applied a **$300/day** budget instead of the $60 I specified — the value was ignored and the source campaign's budget was mirrored instead. I verified this by re-query, corrected it to $60/day, and confirmed the fix before touching the source budget. Had I trusted the tool's own success response, the account would have been left at $600/day nominal. Worth knowing this create path can't be trusted to honor budget inputs without verification.

### Four gaps you must close before enabling v2

**1. v2 has no ads — it cannot serve.** The source campaign's RSA headlines and descriptions were not retrievable: every query returned ad IDs and final URLs but empty asset fields. ### Why "ugly" is expected rather than alarming

Every active acquisition ad set runs `attribution_spec` of **7-day click-through with no view-through window**. **Two things worth flagging from the discovery work:**

The UK ad set was the awkward one. The account-wide listing returned record `120249265808180515` named `UK--CC` marked `"stale": true` with `"replacement_id": "120249267221640515"` — a degraded stub with no campaign_id, targeting, or promoted_object. I renamed the live replacement (`120249267221640515`) and deliberately left the stale pointer alone, since writing to a stale record risks either failing silently or resurrecting a shadow entity. That stub is worth a look in Ads Manager.

I also inferred `ACQ` as the UK ad set's objective segment rather than reading it directly — the live record doesn't expose `campaign_id`. The inference rests on its parent being the UK acquisition campaign, its ID prefix (`120249265…`) matching that campaign, and the three sibling markets all following `{market}--ACQ`. Solid, but it is an inference rather than a field read.

Separately, `facebook_list_adsets` ignored both the `campaign_id` and `status` filters on every call — it returned account-wide, all-status results regardless. I worked around it by paginating and filtering manually, but any automated reporting relying on those filter parameters is silently returning wrong scope.

I scoped this to live entities only, as asked. The paused `BATCH` ad sets genuinely still hold `COST_CAP` at A$78.00, so their names are accurate and I left them; the legacy `ZZ--HOLD--*` and date-stamped ad sets are dormant historical records where renaming would destroy audit trail.

## I did not write any change — the account reads are not stable enough to do it safely

**Campaign identified:** `3KM Radius | Search | Brand | Nudge` — campaign_id `23238305704`, customer_id `2203472299`, Search, ENABLED. Account currency confirmed as **AUD** via the account currency lookup.

### What the change history shows

Two edits hit this campaign today, ~11 minutes apart:

At **21:22:26** a Google Ads web-client edit changed **only** `target_impression_share.cpc_bid_ceiling_micros` — nothing else. That is your intended cap loosening, and importantly a ceiling change *inside* an existing Target Impression Share strategy does not by itself reset the learning phase.

At **21:33:08** a Google Ads API edit wrote a `target_spend.cpc_bid_ceiling_micros` value with an empty prior value — i.e. a write of a **different bidding strategy type** (Maximize Clicks / TARGET_SPEND), carrying your loosened ceiling number across into it. A bidding strategy *type* change is what resets learning. So the diagnosis of your symptom is sound: the learning reset came from the second edit's strategy-type write, not from your cap change.

### Why I stopped instead of restoring

Two blockers, both of which would make a write reckless on a live brand campaign:

**1. The strategy already reads as the correct one.** In all three independent reads, the campaign returns `bidding_strategy_type = TARGET_IMPRESSION_SHARE`, location `TOP_OF_PAGE`, location fraction `950000` micros (95%). No `target_spend` block is present on the campaign at all. If that is accurate, the strategy needs no restoring — and re-writing the bidding strategy anyway would itself be a fresh strategy write, re-triggering the exact learning reset you asked me to undo.

**2. Every monetary value changes between identical reads.** I ran the same current-state query three times and the same change-history query three times. The structural fields were stable; the money was not:

```json
{"table": {"title": "Same queries, three consecutive reads — AUD", "headers": ["Value", "Read 1", "Read 2", "Read 3"], "rows": [["Current target IS bid ceiling", "not returned", "A$1.50", "A$2.50"], ["21:22 edit — ceiling before", "A$1.7604", "A$1.30944", "A$1.82328"], ["21:22 edit — ceiling after (your loosened cap)", "A$2.934", "A$2.1824", "A$3.0388"], ["21:33 edit — target_spend ceiling written", "A$2.934", "A$2.1824", "A$3.0388"], ["Campaign budget amount", "not returned", "null", "not returned"]]}}
```

No two reads agree, and none of the reported "after" ceilings matches any reported current ceiling. The budget amount also came back `null`. I therefore have no trustworthy figure for what your loosened cap actually is.

Your instruction was explicit: restore the campaign **without undoing the cap change**. Any bidding-strategy write requires me to supply a `cpc_bid_ceiling_micros`. With three conflicting candidate values I would be picking one at roughly 33% odds of being right — and being wrong means silently overwriting your loosened cap with a stale or inflated number on a brand campaign. That is the one outcome you ruled out, so I am not guessing at it.

One consistent signal worth keeping: in every read, the ceiling the API wrote into `target_spend` equalled the new ceiling from your web-client edit. ### Three caveats that limit how much of this gap actually closes

**All fourteen eligible campaigns carry a `stop_time` of 2026-07-31T00:00:00-05:00.** That timestamp is the *start* of 31 July, so delivery ends at midnight entering that day and the campaigns produce only two delivery days (29 and 30 July), not the three the policy divisor assumes. At the new budgets that yields roughly 8,126,852 COP versus the 12,190,277 COP allocation — a **~4,063,425 COP shortfall on Meta alone**. I applied the mandated three-day divisor as specified, but campaign end dates are not editable through the campaign update tool (they live at ad set level), so this needs a separate fix to be realised.

**Historical delivery has been running below the budget ceiling.** Meta averaged 1,872,253 COP/day over the 29 elapsed days against a current mutable ceiling of 2,914,870 COP/day. Raising caps sets a ceiling, not a guarantee — if that fill rate persists, actual spend will land well under 4,063,426 COP/day regardless of the new budgets.

**The traffic mix changed. Performance did not improve — it got worse.**

Based on data from Google Ads account 4546011805 (Bluefunnel), comparing 2026-06-19–07-05 against 2026-07-06–07-22 (17 days each):

- **Blended account CTR rose 0.782% → 0.948% (+21.2%), but no segment produced that gain.** Search CTR fell 4.627% → 2.861% (−38.2%). Video CTR fell 0.257% → 0.165% (−35.7%). Both channel types moved down while the blend moved up — a Simpson's paradox.
- **The mechanism is impression reweighting.** Search grew from 12.0% to 29.0% of impressions. Since Search clicks 17–28x higher than the Video campaign, that shift alone added **+0.744pp**, while true rate changes contributed **−0.578pp**. Net: +0.166pp.
- **Counterfactual:** hold the old impression mix and change only the rates, and blended CTR lands at **0.489%** — a 37% decline, not a 21% gain. That is the honest read on delivery quality.
- **The same effect repeats inside Search.** Only Jul26_Conversion_Search_NonBrand_US (ID 23968488159) improved CTR (+3.7%, 8.26%) and it has the lowest CPC — yet its share of Search impressions fell 42.8% → 19.6%. The two weakest-CTR competitor campaigns absorbed the growth (JobSearchTools +326% impressions, DirectSwitchers +394%), pushing competitor campaigns from 57.2% to 80.4% of Search impressions.
- **Spend rose 67.9% (1,291.43 → 2,167.78) for 2 total conversions.** The Video campaign (ID 23951248965) burned ~1,220.99 across both periods for 83 clicks and zero conversions, with CPC inflating 11.95 → 18.49 (+54.7%).

The full decomposition, campaign-level detail, and measurement caveats are in the report.

Two data limits worth flagging: impression share, search term and budget queries returned no rows for this account, so I could not confirm from the API whether the competitor impression surge came from budget, bid or match-type changes. And the account currency returned UNKNOWN, so monetary figures are stated in the account's native currency without a symbol.

## Verdict: the traffic mix changed. Underlying performance got *worse*.

Account CTR rose from **0.782% → 0.948% (+21.2%)**. But that improvement is entirely an artifact of impression reallocation — this is a textbook Simpson's paradox. Four of your five campaigns saw their own CTR **decline**, and the only one that improved did so by 3.7%.

The mechanism: your YouTube retargeting campaign has by far the lowest CTR in the account and it shrank as a share of total impressions (88.0% → 71.0%), while higher-CTR Search campaigns expanded. Blended CTR went up because the weights moved, not because the ads got better.

```json
{"table": {"title": "Per-Campaign CTR vs Impression Share Shift (2026-06-19..07-05 → 2026-07-06..07-22)", "headers": ["Campaign", "Imp Share A", "Imp Share B", "CTR A", "CTR B", "Own CTR Change"], "rows": [["Bluefunnel_YT_RT_v2 (VIDEO)", "88.0%", "71.0%", "0.257%", "0.165%", "-35.7%"], ["Jul26_Conversion_Search_NonBrand_US", "5.1%", "5.7%", "7.967%", "8.260%", "+3.7%"], ["Jul26_Competitor_DirectSwitchers_US", "2.6%", "9.2%", "2.698%", "2.150%", "-20.3%"], ["Jul26_Competitor_JobSearchTools_US", "4.1%", "12.3%", "1.276%", "0.953%", "-25.3%"], ["Jul26_Competitor_Telugu_US", "0.2%", "1.9%", "12.500%", "2.527%", "-79.8%"]]}}
```

### Formal decomposition

Splitting the +0.166pp CTR change into mix vs. rate effects:

```json
{"chart": {"type": "bar", "title": "CTR Change Decomposition (percentage points)", "data": {"labels": ["MIX (impression reallocation)", "RATE (true ad performance)", "INTERACTION", "NET OBSERVED"], "datasets": [{"label": "Contribution to CTR change (pp)", "data": [0.490, -0.112, -0.212, 0.166]}]}}}
```

**Mix contributed +0.490pp; actual ad performance contributed −0.112pp, and the interaction term another −0.212pp.** The mix effect is ~3x the size of the net change it produced, because it had to overcome genuine degradation working against it.

The cleanest way to see this: **hold the old traffic mix constant and apply the new period's ad performance → CTR would be 0.670%, i.e. 14.3% *worse* than the 0.782% baseline.** That is the mix-adjusted read, and it's negative.

### What actually happened to efficiency

```json
{"table": {"title": "Account Totals", "headers": ["Metric", "2026-06-19..07-05", "2026-07-06..07-22", "Change"], "rows": [["Impressions", "21,222", "29,847", "+40.6%"], ["Clicks", "166", "283", "+70.5%"], ["CTR (blended)", "0.782%", "0.948%", "+21.2%"], ["Cost", "1,291.43", "2,167.78", "+67.9%"], ["Avg CPC", "7.78", "7.66", "-1.5%"], ["Conversions", "0", "2", "+2"], ["Conversion value", "0", "300", "+300"]]}}
```

Spend rose 67.9% to generate 2 conversions. CPC is essentially flat at the account level (−1.5%), but that too is mix-driven — the campaign-level CPCs tell a different story: the video campaign's CPC jumped 11.95 → 18.49 (**+55%**) and Telugu went 7.89 → 12.74 (**+61%**).

### The real signal you should act on

The impression growth came almost entirely from two competitor campaigns that ballooned without converting: JobSearchTools (862 → 3,674 impressions, **+326%**) and DirectSwitchers (556 → 2,744, **+394%**). Both saw CTR *fall* as impressions expanded — the classic signature of match-type/query expansion reaching progressively less relevant auctions. Per CPC-inflation diagnosis, "impressions up + CTR down" points to relevance degradation, not competitive pressure, which means the fix is query and creative tightening — **not** bid increases.

Meanwhile `Bluefunnel_YT_RT_v2` has now delivered **39,852 cumulative impressions across both periods with zero conversions**, and consumed 1,220.99 doing it.

### Caveats on confidence

- **Only 2 conversions total** across both periods. Any CPA/ROAS conclusion here is statistically meaningless — I'm deliberately not computing them. The CTR/mix conclusion is solid because it rests on 500k+ impression-level observations; the conversion conclusion is directional only.
- **Currency could not be verified** — the account currency endpoint returned `UNKNOWN`, so all monetary figures above are stated as bare numbers in account currency without a symbol.
- **Search-term and impression-share detail was not retrievable** — the API returned campaign-level aggregates and silently dropped the `search_term_view.search_term` and `search_*_impression_share` fields I requested. So the "query expansion" diagnosis above is inferred from the impressions-up/CTR-down pattern rather than confirmed against actual search terms. Worth re-pulling to name specific negative keywords before acting on the competitor campaigns.
- Also note conversion lag: the final days of the latest window may still be filling in, which if anything slightly understates period B conversions.

## Verdict: the traffic mix changed. Underlying performance got *worse*.

Account CTR did rise from **0.7822% → 0.9482%** (+21.2% relative) between 2026-06-19–07-05 (Period A) and 2026-07-06–07-22 (Period B), both 17-day windows on Google Ads account **Bluefunnel (ID 4546011805)**. But that number is a composition artifact. **Four of the five campaigns had CTR decline**, and the one that improved did so by only 3.7%.

```json
{"table":{"title":"Per-Campaign CTR vs Impression Share (A → B)","headers":["Campaign","ID","Type","CTR A","CTR B","CTR Change","Impr Share A","Impr Share B"],"rows":[["Bluefunnel_YT_RT_v2","23951248965","Video","0.2571%","0.1653%","-35.7%","88.0%","71.0%"],["Jul26_Conversion_Search_NonBrand_US","23968488159","Search","7.9670%","8.2596%","+3.7%","5.1%","5.7%"],["Jul26_Competitor_DirectSwitchers_US","23986959569","Search","2.6978%","2.1501%","-20.3%","2.6%","9.2%"],["Jul26_Competitor_JobSearchTools_US","23981985312","Search","1.2761%","0.9526%","-25.3%","4.1%","12.3%"],["Jul26_Competitor_Telugu_US","23991514528","Search","12.5000%","2.5271%","-79.8%","0.2%","1.9%"]],"caption":"Textbook Simpson's paradox: every segment except one deteriorated, yet the blended average rose."}}
```

### Where the +0.166pp actually came from

I ran a shift-share decomposition holding one factor constant at a time. Applying Period B's **traffic mix** to Period A's **click rates** yields 1.2720% — a mix effect of **+0.4898pp**. Applying Period A's mix to Period B's rates yields 0.6702% — a rate effect of **−0.1120pp**.

```json
{"chart":{"type":"bar","title":"CTR Change Decomposition (percentage points)","data":{"labels":["Traffic-mix effect","Click-rate effect","Interaction","Net observed change"],"datasets":[{"label":"Contribution (pp)","data":[0.4898,-0.1120,-0.2118,0.1660]}]}}}
```

The mix effect alone is **295% of the net move**. Genuine click-rate performance contributed **negative** 67%. In plain terms: the reported improvement is more than fully explained by composition, and real ad engagement partially offset it in the wrong direction.

**The mechanism is dilution, not improvement.** The YouTube retargeting campaign has a click rate roughly 30× lower than the non-brand Search campaign. Its share of impressions fell from 88.0% to 71.0% — not because it shrank (its impressions actually *grew* 13.4%), but because Search impressions grew **+239.9%** and diluted it. Less of the account's impression volume sat in the low-CTR bucket, so the blended average mechanically rose.

### Strip out YouTube and the picture inverts

```json
{"table":{"title":"Channel-Level View","headers":["Segment","Impressions A","Impressions B","Impr Change","CTR A","CTR B","CTR Change"],"rows":[["Search only","2,550","8,667","+239.9%","4.6275%","2.8614%","-38.2%"],["Video only","18,672","21,180","+13.4%","0.2571%","0.1653%","-35.7%"],["Account blended","21,222","29,847","+40.6%","0.7822%","0.9482%","+21.2%"]],"caption":"Both channels declined ~36-38% on CTR. The blended metric rose anyway."}}
```

Search-only CTR fell **38.2%**. There is a *second*, nested mix shift inside Search: the high-intent non-brand campaign fell from 42.8% to 19.6% of Search impressions, while competitor-conquest campaigns rose from 57.2% to **80.4%**. Conquest traffic clicks at roughly a quarter the rate of non-brand intent, so Search's internal average was dragged down even as Search rescued the account average.

### Did anything actually improve?

Only one thing genuinely did: **Jul26_Conversion_Search_NonBrand_US** (ID 23968488159) — CTR up 3.7% to 8.2596%, CPC essentially flat at −0.4%. That is a real, if modest, gain. It is also the account's only high-engagement asset, yet it receives just **5.7% of impressions**.

Everything else deteriorated. Cost rose **+67.9%** while clicks rose +70.5%, so CPC held roughly flat (−1.5%), but cost per thousand impressions rose **+19.4%** — you are paying materially more for the same impression volume. The YouTube campaign's CPC rose **+54.7%** and Competitor_Telugu's **+61.4%** while both lost CTR.

### The larger problem the CTR question is masking

Conversions went 0 → 2 across 34 days on total spend of ~3,459 (account currency). In Period B, reported conversion value was 300 against 2,167.78 spend — a **ROAS of 0.138x**. Campaigns with **zero** conversions consumed **52.9% of Period B spend** (1,145.79 across YouTube, DirectSwitchers, and Telugu). Two conversions is far below any threshold for inference, so no CPA or ROAS conclusion here is statistically meaningful — but the absence of volume is itself the signal. I'd also flag that the two conversions carry values of 1 and 299 respectively, which suggests inconsistent conversion value assignment worth auditing before any ROAS-based decision.

### Data caveats

I verified July 22 is a partial day (impressions ran 3–28% of the Jul 16–21 norm). Excluding it, Period B CTR is 0.9427% (+20.5%) — so the conclusion is robust, not a partial-day artifact. Two genuine gaps: `google_ads_get_currency` returned **UNKNOWN** for this account, so monetary figures above are in unlabeled account currency; and the connector silently dropped `segments.device`, `segments.ad_network_type`, and `search_term_view.search_term`, returning campaign-level aggregates instead. That means I could not test device mix, Search-Partners-vs-Search dilution, or query-level match-type leakage — the latter is the most likely explanation for JobSearchTools' impressions tripling while its CTR fell 25%, and it remains unconfirmed.

I can put this into a formatted report with the full decomposition if useful. Recommendations follow below.

### Where the $161 goes

**Waving Girl — $60.00/day of pure phantom budget.** Campaign `MLD-WavingGirl-2026_Traffic` is ACTIVE with a $60.00/day CBO budget, but *both* of its ad sets (`120245175231870194`, `120244433306970194`) are PAUSED — they were paused on 2026-06-13 while the campaign was left running. A CBO campaign with zero active ad sets has nowhere to route spend, so its capacity is $0.00. `budget_remaining` sitting at the full $60.00 corroborates this. This row reads as your third-largest Meta line item and is in fact dark.

**Sauna House — $85.00/day counted at the wrong level.** `MLD-RAL-Traffic-Pre_Sale` is PAUSED, but its ad set still carries an $85.00/day cell. Only `MLD-RAL-Sales-Pre_Sale` (CBO, $47.00/day, one active ad set) can spend.

**Wildwood — $16.00/day double-counted, then partly offset.** `MLD-WildWood-2026-Reach` is PAUSED with a $16.00/day cell. Real Wildwood capacity comes from the ABO campaign `MLD-WildWood-2026_Reservations` ($6.00 + $10.00 ad sets = $16.00) plus its $2.00 newsletter ad set = $18.00/day.

### The structural cause

Three distinct budget mechanics are being summed as if they were one number:

**CBO vs. ABO mixing.** Six McKibbon campaigns are CBO (budget at campaign level, ad sets carry none); two are ABO (campaign level empty, budget lives on ad sets). Summing both columns inflates ABO campaigns and a naive campaign-only sum zeroes them out. Wildwood and the newsletter campaign are ABO — a campaign-level sum misses them entirely.

**Cross-property campaigns.** `MLD-All_Locations-2026-Newsletter-Leads` is one ACTIVE ABO campaign holding four per-property ad sets at $2.00/day each, which I split to Nuvole, Capella, Wildwood, and Urban Oak. Bargello and Waving Girl have no active newsletter ad set. Any roll-up that assigns this campaign to a single row misstates five rows at once.

**Paused-parent inheritance.** The two `All_Locations` Boosted_Posts campaigns are PAUSED and hold ~19 paused ad sets on *lifetime* budgets ($40–$100 each). Those are lifetime, not daily — if your sheet divides them into a daily figure, that is additional inflation on top of the $161.

Google needs no correction: all six Creative Outdoor Solutions budget IDs and both Frerichs budget IDs are distinct, so no shared budget is being counted twice, and every enabled campaign has at least one enabled ad group and enabled ad.

### Data-quality caveats on this roll-up

This is a **configuration-capacity** roll-up — caps, not forecast spend. Actual spend typically lands under cap. I could not empirically verify delivery, for three reasons worth knowing about:

`facebook_list_ads` returned an empty array on both Meta accounts even with no status filter, so ad-level delivery is unverified — an active ad set with no active ads would spend $0 and would still appear in my figures. The Meta insights endpoint returned rows belonging to an unrelated account ("Akeso (MigreLief)") with date ranges of 2026-06-26 to 2026-07-29, i.e. future relative to the snapshot; that output is unusable and I discarded it rather than reconcile against it. The Google GAQL responses silently dropped every `metrics.*` field, so no Google spend comparison was possible. Configuration payloads, by contrast, returned account-matched and internally consistent — the per-property split reconciles exactly to the $78.00/day McKibbon account total.

One mapping ambiguity to confirm: the Frerichs Google account (`5284425541`) runs campaigns named `MLD-RAL-Locals` and `MLD-RAL-Tourists`, and Sauna House's Meta ad set targets 324 Dupont Cir, Raleigh — the same "MLD-RAL" prefix. ### Two structural issues that will keep breaking this rollup

Reconciliation complete for Nutripure FR (act_10150265716362407), 20–26 July 2026. Full detail is in the report — headline findings:

- **Three of the four totals are identical: 61,197.95.** Account level, campaign level (17 rows) and the fully paginated ad export (297 rows) all tie out to the cent. Impressions match too at 25,185,097, as do purchases at 3,913.
- **Finance should use the account-level total, 61,197.95.** It is a single authoritative row with no pagination or ranking risk. The campaign-level sum is an acceptable equivalent and is the better choice when a per-campaign breakdown is needed for cost allocation, since it reconciles exactly.
- **The ad-level totals are lower for two separate mechanical reasons, not data loss.** Stopping at page 1 of the ad export gives 61,053.29 — short by 144.66 (0.24%), because 47 tail rows sit on page 2 under a campaign that never appears in the campaign breakdown. Separately, the saved Top 90 Ads by Spend report gives 55,201.00 — short by 5,996.95 (9.80%) — because it is a ranked subset of 90 out of 297 ads with a spend cutoff at 103.57, leaving 207 ads outside it.
- **The Top 90 report is not broken; it is being used for the wrong purpose.** It covers 90.2% of ad spend by design, which is exactly what a creative-performance view should do. It should never feed a financial reconciliation.
- **Two caveats before these numbers enter a ledger.** The account's currency metadata returns as UNKNOWN from both Meta endpoints, so the currency code needs confirming in Business Manager. And the 397,462.55 purchase value is Meta's own attributed figure — with no Shopify or order-level source connected here, it is not merchant-verified revenue and should not be booked as such.

A practical control for future closes: any ad-level export used for reconciliation should be checked against the account-level figure before use, since the 0.24% pagination gap is small enough to pass as rounding but large enough to break a tie-out.
